# Build transcript

Project 3 (Quick Dresser, weather outfit app): implementation session, C0 through C8b and the wrap-up, 2026-10-10 to 2026-10-12. Chat messages only. The Agent's multiple-choice questions show their options, and the Developer's answers appear as Developer messages. Agent tool actions (file edits, tests, browser checks, art rounds, exports, commits and deploys) are reflected in `spec.md`, `plan.md`, `reference/screens/`, the code and the commit history.

### Developer · 2026-10-10 22:10 CDT

Phase 1 of Quick Dresser is complete and approved. research.md, spec.md (R1–R39), and plan.md (checkpoints C0–C12) are the source of truth. We're now starting Phase 2: the build. Read AGENTS.md, brief.md, research.md, spec.md, and plan.md in full, then run `git status` and `git log --oneline` before doing anything else.

## Phase 2 deliverables

1. A working app built through tested checkpoints, with commit history, and with spec.md and plan.md updated to record revisions made during the build
2. The app deployed at a public HTTPS address (https://rg-sureshkumar-goat.github.io/weather-outfit-app/), matching the approved spec
3. Usability-testing notes from three peers in plan.md, with findings and the resulting improvement
4. A completed debrief conversation (I'll add debrief.md later; it runs in its own session)
5. Transcripts from every implementation and debrief session, saved in transcripts/
6. At the end: every project file committed, and the Git commit log exported to commit-log.txt with commit IDs, dates, and full commit messages, and no code or diffs
7. The log committed and every commit pushed to GitHub. I'll then download the repo as a ZIP from GitHub (Code → Download ZIP) for submission

## How we build

- Follow plan.md's checkpoints in order, one at a time. Don't start the next checkpoint until I've verified the current one.
- Before each checkpoint, give me a short summary: what you'll build, which R-numbers it covers, which files it touches, and how you'll prove it's done (the plan's "Done when" line). Then wait for my go-ahead.
- After each checkpoint, run `npm test`, check the screens in the browser at 375 px and 1280 px, and report the result for each R-number with screenshots or test output. Don't just say it works. List anything that fell short.
- Commit only after I've verified the checkpoint. Use clear messages that name the checkpoint and R-numbers (e.g. "C1: recommendation engine, rules and picks (R7–R14, R29)"), push, and confirm that the GitHub Actions deploy succeeded.
- Art rounds (C2, C5, C7, C10): show me the art and wait for my approval before building on it. The character must be original, not a copy of Bitmoji.
- When a decision is mine (font, art style, a layout question the wireframes don't answer, a rule edge the spec doesn't cover), ask ONE focused question at a time and give me options with trade-offs. Don't decide silently.
- Steps only I can do: turning on GitHub Pages, phone and laptop checks of the deployed site, the Device tests table entries, peer testing, and approvals. Tell me exactly what to do, then wait. Never check off approvals or verification items for me.

## Keeping the documents current

- If something in the spec turns out not to work, or a better approach comes up, stop and tell me what you found and what you'd change. Once I agree, record it in spec.md → Revisions (when the intended result changes) or plan.md → Revisions (when the plan changes), with the date and the evidence. If a layout changes materially, update the screen wireframes too.
- Fill in the "Project" section of AGENTS.md in C0 (run, test, deploy) and keep it accurate.
- Keep plan.md's checklist current as we go.

## Scope

- Build only what spec.md specifies. Respect its Out of scope list. Don't add features.
- The "Core deploy" milestone (C0–C6: the full app except the wardrobe, live on Pages) is the top priority. If time or technical risk threatens later checkpoints (cut-out, selfie reading, art volume), raise it with me early and suggest options. Don't cut or simplify quietly.
- Never commit secrets or my private photos. test-garments/, test-selfie/ and *.HEIC must stay ignored. Check before every commit.

## Usability testing (after C12 is deployed)

Help me prepare the Usability testing section of plan.md: purpose, 3–4 realistic tasks, non-leading prompts, and a consistent note format. I'll run three peer sessions on the deployed app. Then help me record each one with a non-identifying label, keeping observations separate from interpretations, and summarize the strongest findings. I'll choose the improvement. Then add it to the checklist, update spec.md if the intended result changes, implement it, verify it, redeploy, and record it under Revisions.

## Sessions and transcripts

The build will span several sessions. At the end of every session, ask me to enter `save transcript`, then save the full session as transcripts/build-YYYY-MM-DD_HHMMSS.md with messages labeled Developer and Agent, and commit it. At the start of each later session, re-read plan.md and `git log` to find where we left off.

## Finish

When the build, testing, and improvement are done and the debrief transcript is saved, check every brief deliverable with me. Then commit everything, export commit-log.txt (for example `git log --no-patch --date=iso --format='commit %H%nDate: %ad%n%n%B%n'`, which gives full messages and no diffs), commit it, push, and confirm with `git status` that main is clean and in sync with origin.

## Start

1. Confirm in a few lines what Quick Dresser is and the checkpoint order, so I know you've read the documents.
2. Ask me this question first: .gitignore excludes reference/feasibility/ with the note "in the submission zip only." But the submission ZIP comes from GitHub, which only contains pushed files, so those pages would be missing. Should we commit that folder (note its size), keep relying on the artifact links in research.md, or do something else?
3. Then propose C0 and wait for my go-ahead.

### Agent · 2026-10-10 22:11 CDT

Read all five documents and the git state; now checking tool versions so the C0 proposal is grounded.

**Quick Dresser, as I understand it.** It's a static web app built with Vite and plain JavaScript and hosted on GitHub Pages. For a US college student it picks one outfit for the hardest weather between 9 am and 9 pm, today or any of the next 6 days, using Open-Meteo data.

- The outfit comes from the User's own photographed clothes, cut out on the device. Starter items fill any gaps.
- It's shown on an original 2D avatar set up from a selfie that's read on the device and then discarded.
- Five feels-like bands set the outfit, plus reminders for umbrella, sunscreen, water, layer for later, wind and snow.
- One pure `buildState()` drives the avatar, weather icon, summary, reminders and Wearing list.

**Checkpoint order:**
- C0 setup and deploy pipeline
- C1 recommendation engine
- C2 art round 1: character and layers, plus your font choice
- C3 location
- C4 Today
- C5 avatar review and first run (art round 2)
- C6 Info
- **Core deploy**, with your device tests
- C7 Closet and Add item (art round 3)
- C8 garment cut-out
- C9 selfie reading
- C10 art round 4: icons and scenery
- C11 accessibility pass
- C12 release candidate
- Then peer testing and one improvement

Committing them would publish that photo in the public repo, and it would stay in the history. The selfie test page doesn't contain your selfie.

## C0 proposal: setup and deploy pipeline

**What I'll build**
- **Vite + Vitest project:**
  - `package.json` with `dev`, `build`, `preview` and `test` scripts.
  - Exact versions: Vite 8.3.4, Vitest 5.0.3, `@mediapipe/tasks-vision` 1.0.1.
  - `vite.config.js` with `base: '/weather-outfit-app/'`. Pages serves the site from that subpath, so without it every asset returns 404.
- **Placeholder page:** `index.html` and `src/main.js` with a "Quick Dresser, coming soon" page. No app features.
- **Folders from the plan:** `src/{weather,recommend,content,store,art,screens,vision}/`, `public/` and `tests/`, each holding a `.gitkeep` until real files arrive.
- **MediaPipe copy script:** `scripts/copy-mediapipe.mjs` copies the runtime from `node_modules` into `public/mediapipe/`. It runs automatically before `dev` and `build`, and the copied files stay out of git.
- **Deploy workflow:** `.github/workflows/deploy.yml` runs on every push to `main`. It installs, runs `npm test`, builds and publishes `dist/` to Pages. If a test fails, nothing deploys.
- **One smoke test:** `tests/smoke.test.js` checks that the build's base path matches the Pages path. The real rule tests start in C1.
- **Housekeeping:**
  - `.gitignore` gains `node_modules/`, `dist/` and `public/mediapipe/`.
  - The Project section of `AGENTS.md` gets how to run, test and deploy.
  - I'll tick C0 in `plan.md` only after you've verified it.

**R-numbers.** None is fully met in C0. It lays groundwork for R38 (public HTTPS URL on Pages) and R37 (the runtime hosted with the app, not loaded from Google).

3. **Your step before I push:** on GitHub, open the repo's Settings → Pages → Build and deployment and set Source to **GitHub Actions**. Right now the URL returns 404 and the repo reports no Pages site.
4. I push, then confirm the Actions run succeeded through GitHub's public API. The `gh` command-line tool isn't installed on this Mac, so I'll use `curl`. Then I'll show `curl -I` returning HTTP 200 over HTTPS and a screenshot of the live URL.

**Shortfalls I already expect:** none. The Device tests table begins at Core deploy, after C6, not here.

### Agent · 2026-10-10 22:38 CDT

This doesn't affect C0, which is still waiting on your local check and the Pages switch. It does change the spec, so I'll settle the details with you before building anything. It touches:
- C2, the character art
- C5, the review screen
- C7, the garment templates

So it needs to be decided before C2.

**What bears on the decision:**
- **Body shape is currently out of scope.** spec.md lists "body-shape options" under Out of scope, and art volume is the plan's top risk. A second body shape means every garment has to be drawn to fit both bodies, so the 12 templates become 24.
- **There's no authoritative guideline for how a gender should look.** Avatar apps like Bitmoji typically use gender to set the body and face starting point and the default options, while keeping hair, facial hair and clothes open to everyone.
- **Gender should be chosen, not read from the selfie.** MediaPipe's face models don't classify gender, and guessing it risks getting it wrong. The User picks it.
- **Small privacy change.** Gender would be one more avatar value stored only on the device, so R34 and the privacy text on Info change slightly.

First question: what should choosing a gender change? You can pick more than one.

**Question (Gender):** What should the gender choice change about the avatar and outfits?

- **Starting defaults:** Sets the starting hairstyle for hand setup and whether facial hair starts on. Every control stays open to everyone. No new art, low cost.
- **Face details:** Small face differences on the same body, such as brow shape, lashes and lip shape. About 3 to 6 extra face layers, added in C2. Moderate cost.
- **Body silhouette:** A second body shape, for example shoulder and hip proportions. All 12 garment templates must fit both bodies, so 24 drawings. It moves body shape into scope and is the highest risk to the schedule.
- **Starter outfits:** A separate set of starter outfits per gender, for example with skirts. Changes recommendations, not just the avatar. Color data only, about 15 more outfits per extra set.

### Agent · 2026-10-10 22:45 CDT

Gender gets its own first step, and setup becomes **Gender → Selfie → Review avatar → Location → Today**. That covers the open questions. Here's the change written up as a short spec for you to check before I touch any documents.

## Change spec: gender in setup (proposed new R40)

**Goal.** In the first setup step the User picks Woman or Man. Gender sets the avatar's starting body, face and default hairstyle, and decides which set of starter outfits is used. It limits nothing.

**Behavior**
- **Gender step:** a new first screen asks "Woman" or "Man". One must be picked before Continue works. There's no third option. That was your choice, and the revision note will record the trade-off.
- **What gender sets** (proposed values, change any you like):

  | | Woman | Man |
  |---|---|---|
  | Body | Body A | Body B |
  | Face details | Set A | Set B |
  | Hairstyle for hand setup | Long straight | Short crop |
  | Facial hair at start | None | None |
  | Starter outfits | Woman set (below) | The current 15 |

  Facial hair starts off for both because choosing it for someone is presumptuous. Adding it is one tap.
- **Nothing limited:** the review screen gains **Gender**, **Body** and **Face** controls, and each can be changed on its own. Changing Gender there resets Body, Face and the starter set to that gender's values. Skin, hair, facial hair and glasses stay as they were. Every hairstyle, facial-hair option, glasses choice and closet item type stays open to both.
- **Selfie:** the hairstyle read from a selfie overrides the gender default. Gender is never read from the selfie.
- **Storage:** gender is saved on the device with the avatar values and never sent anywhere. "Edit avatar" can change it. Doing so switches the starter set, and only slots holding starter items are re-picked (R14).

**Draft Woman starter set.** Skirts are only allowed on Hot and Warm days by the garment table. Dresses stay out of scope.

| Band | Outfit 1 | Outfit 2 | Outfit 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hot | White tee · denim skirt · white sneakers | Coral short-sleeve button-up · white shorts · white sneakers | Lavender tee · khaki skirt · grey sneakers |
| Warm | Sage tee · light jeans · white sneakers | White short-sleeve button-up · navy skirt · white sneakers | Rose long-sleeve shirt · light jeans · white sneakers |
| Chilly | Cream sweater · light jeans · white sneakers · denim jacket | White long-sleeve shirt · black pants · grey sneakers · tan light jacket | Lavender hoodie · dark jeans · white sneakers · olive light jacket |
| Cold | Cream sweater · dark jeans · brown boots · camel coat | Rose hoodie · black pants · black boots · charcoal coat | Navy long-sleeve shirt · light jeans · brown boots · burgundy coat |
| Freezing | Grey sweater · black pants · black boots · camel coat | Cream hoodie · dark jeans · brown boots · burgundy coat | Navy sweater · khaki pants · brown boots · forest-green coat |

The yellow hooded rain jacket is shared by both sets.

**Done when:**
- On a fresh install the gender step comes first, and Continue stays disabled until a gender is picked.
- Man, then hand setup, shows Body B, Face B and Short crop. Woman shows Body A, Face A and Long straight.
- Changing Body on the review screen changes only the body.
- With an empty closet, Hot days use the Woman set for Woman and the Man set for Man.
- No network request carries the gender.

**Document edits after you approve:**
- **spec.md:**
  - Add R40.
  - R22: the setup order.
  - R24: the Gender, Body and Face controls.
  - R14: re-pick when gender changes.
  - R34 and R35: gender stored on the device, and cleared by "Delete all my data".
  - Content variation: two starter sets.
  - Assets: two bodies, two face sets, each garment fitted to both bodies.
  - Out of scope: body shapes beyond two, and gender options beyond Woman and Man.
  - Revisions, dated 2026-10-10.
  - Wireframes: a new gender screen (P9, L9) and updated review screens (P8, L8).
- **plan.md:**
  - C1: the engine takes gender and both starter sets.
  - C2: two bodies, two face sets, and the 5 starter templates fitted to both.
  - C5: the gender step and the new controls.
  - C7: 12 templates fitted to both bodies and 30 starter outfits.
  - The art-volume risk.
  - Revisions.
- **research.md:** one Revisions line saying the art estimate there is superseded by spec.md.

**Schedule risk.** C2 and C5 are part of Core deploy, and both get heavier, so Core deploy will land later. Whether each garment is drawn twice or drawn once with adjustable shoulder and hip points is a separate decision I'll bring to you when I propose C2.

Before building anything, I need to know which kind of iPhone app you mean, because the options differ a lot. What I know so far:
- **The brief requires a browser-based app**, deployed at an HTTPS address. A native iOS app would be a different product:
  - It needs Xcode and your Apple ID.
  - Free installs from Xcode stop working after 7 days.
  - TestFlight needs the paid Apple Developer Program ($99 a year).
- **An iPhone can install a web app** from Safari with Share → Add to Home Screen. It gets its own icon, opens like an app, and picks up each new deploy without being downloaded again.
- **Camera and location only work over HTTPS**, so the deployed GitHub Pages site supports them and the Mac's local dev server over Wi-Fi doesn't.
- **C0 already sets this up:** once Pages is on and C0 is pushed, every verified checkpoint goes live at the URL automatically.

**Question (iPhone app):** What kind of iPhone app do you want for testing during the build?

- **Home Screen web app (Recommended):** Installed from Safari with Add to Home Screen. It opens from its own icon and always runs the latest deploy. Meets the brief. I'd add a small web app manifest and a placeholder icon now; the final icon comes in C10.
- **Native iOS app:** A wrapper built in Xcode (for example Capacitor) and installed on your phone. Free installs expire every 7 days, TestFlight costs $99 a year, and it moves away from the brief's browser-based app. Large extra work and risk.
- **Just Safari:** No install: open or bookmark the Pages URL in Safari. No extra work, but no icon and Safari's toolbars stay visible.

- **Local HTTPS certificate:** I serve the build over HTTPS on your Wi-Fi with a certificate made on the Mac (mkcert). You install and trust it on the iPhone yourself in Settings, and everything works before any commit. That certificate can vouch for any site if its key leaks.

I've ticked C0 as done in plan.md, now that you've verified it on your Home Screen. That tick will go in with the C1 commit.

## C1 proposal: recommendation engine

C1 is the logic only, with no screens yet. It turns an Open-Meteo forecast, the closet, the avatar and saved picks into **one recommendation state**.

**What I'll build**

| File | Does |
|---|---|
| `src/weather/openMeteo.js` | Builds the forecast request (coordinates rounded to 2 decimals; °F, mph, inches, local time zone, 7 days, the spec's fields) and turns the response into days and hours. Missing values stay marked as missing. |
| `src/recommend/window.js` | Takes the 13 hours from 9 am to 9 pm local time, rounded to whole numbers, and works out the highest and lowest feels-like and their times, the peak rain chance, UV and gusts, and snowfall. |
| `src/recommend/rules.js` | Band, reminders and condition icon. Missing-data notes, and marking a day with no forecast as unavailable. |
| `src/recommend/outfits.js` | Which garment types suit each band, the outer-layer and rain-jacket rules, and building at least 3 distinct candidate outfits. |
| `src/recommend/picks.js` | Picks the outfit and each wording at random, separately (the random source can be swapped in tests). Shuffle. Re-picks only the part a change affects. |
| `src/recommend/state.js` | `buildState()`, a pure function: the same inputs always give the same state. |
| `src/content/` | Garment types by band, both starter sets plus the rain jacket, and all wordings with fill-ins, copied from spec.md. |
| `src/store/picks.js` | Saves picks on the device for the current location only. They're cleared when the location changes or the date passes, using the location's own clock. |
| `tests/fixtures/` | Real Open-Meteo responses from 6 places: Austin, Seattle, Chicago, Minneapolis, Anchorage and Honolulu. The plan asks for at least 4. |
| `tests/*.test.js` | Rule tests at every threshold edge, built from small test forecasts, plus tests against the real fixtures. |

**R-numbers**
- **Covered by tests:** R1 (the request and reading the response), R7, R8, R9, R10, R11, R12, R13, R14, R29 and R40 (starter set by gender, and re-picking on a gender change).
- **Engine part only:** R5 (Today keeps its 9 am–9 pm outfit after 9 pm).
- The on-screen and network-log checks for these come in C4.

**How I read R9 and R11** (say if you read them differently):
- Starter outfits stay whole.
- Your own items replace the matching slot.
- With an empty closet, each band offers exactly its 3 starter outfits.
- With one tee, Hot gives 3 outfits: your tee with each starter outfit's bottom and shoes.
- Once your closet can make 3 outfits on its own, starters drop out.

**Rule edges the spec doesn't settle.** These are yours to decide. After your go-ahead I'll ask them one at a time, each with options, and record your answers in spec.md:
1. **Layer for later:** which jacket the reminder names, and how it "matches the outfit".
2. **Rain and wind on a Cold or Freezing day:** does the rain jacket replace the warm coat, go over it, or does only the reminder change?
3. **Snow days:** are boots required, or just allowed?
4. **Wind and rain together:** does the plain Wind reminder still show alongside "Wind and rain"?
5. **Icon ties:** which condition wins when two are equally frequent, or when rain and snow both reach 3 hours?

**Small choices I'll make unless you object:**
- Times in wordings read "3 pm".
- Reminders keep the order of the spec's wording table.
- A day with no feels-like data at all counts as unavailable.

**How I'll prove it's done** (plan: "Done when")
- `npm test` output showing the tests for:
  - every R8 threshold edge (84 °F is Warm and 85 °F is Hot; 39% rain gives no umbrella and 40% does; and so on)

  - 3 distinct starter outfits per band for each gender
  - a gender change re-picking only starter slots

- A worked example: the state each real fixture produces.
- **Screens:** C1 changes nothing on screen, so the 375 px and 1280 px checks would only show the unchanged placeholder. I'll report that rather than pass it off as a check.

**Commit after you verify:** "C1: recommendation engine, rules and picks (R1, R7–R14, R29, R40)", plus the plan.md tick and any spec.md updates from your answers.

Starting C1 with the five rule questions, one at a time.

**Question 1, Layer for later.** This reminder fires when the outfit suits the warmest hour but the coldest hour drops below 70 °F into a colder band. It names a jacket to bring: a light jacket if the evening is Chilly, a warm coat if it's Cold or Freezing. Your own items come first, then starters. The open part is how it "matches the outfit".

**Question (Later layer):** How should the 'layer for later' reminder pick the jacket so it matches the outfit?

- **Neutral colors first (Recommended):** Picks at random among jackets of the right weight in neutral colors (low saturation, such as navy, black, grey, tan, denim, olive or camel), because neutrals go with anything. Falls back to any jacket of that weight if none is neutral. Works the same for your items and starters. Moderate effort.
- **Starter pairing:** Uses the jacket from the colder band's starter outfit with the same number, for example Hot outfit 1 → Chilly outfit 1's denim jacket. Simple, and starters are already styled. Your own jackets get no matching logic and are picked at random.
- **Any jacket, at random:** Any jacket of the right weight, at random and saved with the day's picks. No color logic at all. Simplest, but it can clash with the outfit.

### Agent · 2026-10-10 23:33 CDT

**Question (Icon ties):** How should the condition icon break ties?

- **Most severe wins (Recommended):** Fixed order: storm > snow > rain > fog > cloudy > partly cloudy > clear. 3 storm hours beat 8 rain hours. Fits the app's rule of planning for the hardest part of the day. Simple and predictable.
- **Most hours, then severity:** Among wet conditions with 3 or more hours, the one with the most hours wins. Severity only breaks exact ties. More representative of the day, but a 3-hour storm could be shown as rain.
- **Earliest wins:** Ties go to whichever condition comes first in the day, matching what you'd see on the walk to class. Ignores severity.

### Agent · 2026-10-10 23:47 CDT

They're empty files I added in C0. Git only stores files, not folders, so an empty folder never reaches GitHub. Putting an empty file named `.gitkeep` inside a folder is a common convention to make the folder show up anyway. The name has no special meaning to Git.

I used them so the folder layout from plan.md would exist in the repo before any code did.

Five of those folders now hold real files, so their placeholders do nothing:
- `src/weather/`
- `src/recommend/`
- `src/content/`
- `src/store/`
- `tests/fixtures/`

Removing them changes nothing in the app or the tests; it just tidies up. The other three, in `src/art/`, `src/screens/` and `src/vision/`, are still needed until those folders get files in later checkpoints. I'd remove the five, but leaving them is harmless.

Should I remove them in the C1 commit, and do you approve committing and pushing C1?

**Question (Carry over):** Which qualities of the reference should the character carry over (in an original 2D form)?

- **Big-head proportions:** A large, friendly head, about one fifth of the height, on a slimmer body, so the face reads well at phone size. The research preview was closer to one seventh.
- **Expressive face:** Large eyes, defined brows and a wide smile, so the character feels cheerful. Drawn in my own style, not Bitmoji's eyes or mouth.
- **Soft shading and folds:** Rounded, soft-shaded look: shadow and highlight shapes, fabric folds, ribbed cuffs and hems. More art work per garment than flat color.
- **Relaxed pose:** A natural stance (slight lean, one hand in a pocket) instead of standing straight with arms down. The pose has to work for every garment, so it adds some fitting work.

**Question 3, the font:**

**Question (Font):** Which font should Quick Dresser use?

- **A · Nunito (Recommended):** Soft rounded ends, a full range of weights from light to extra bold, and the most legible at small sizes (I, l and 1 are easy to tell apart). Friendly without being childish. One file covers headings and body.
- **B · Quicksand:** Geometric and airy, with weights up to bold. Its thinner strokes make small labels and reminder text harder to read, especially on a sky backdrop.
- **C · Fredoka:** The most playful, with chunky round letters. Great for headings and the big temperature, but heavy for longer reminder text. Could pair with Nunito for body text, at the cost of a second font file.
- **D · Varela Round:** Clean and simple, but it comes in only one weight with no bold, so headings and labels can only stand out by size.

## C3 proposal: location setup (P2, L2)

**What I'll build**
- **Search (R2):** you type a US city or ZIP code.
  - Results come from Open-Meteo's place search, limited to the US, and show as "City, ST".
  - "Toronto" shows "No US place matches."
  - The text you typed stays in the field if the service fails.
- **"Use my location" (R3):**
  - The browser asks for permission, and the coordinates are rounded to 2 decimals.
  - Each tap makes exactly one reverse lookup with OpenStreetMap's Nominatim to get the place name and check it's in the US, with no repeats or background lookups.
  - Clear messages cover a denied permission (with how to turn it on), a non-US position ("This app works for US locations only") and a failed lookup with Retry. The search field stays ready in every case.
- **Saved location (R4):** one location, saved on this device and restored on the next visit. Choosing a new location replaces it and clears the saved picks (R14).
- **The P2/L2 screen** laid out per the wireframes, with a simple placeholder where the decorative map goes (final art in C10), and a minimal app shell to reach it until C4 builds Today.

**R-numbers:**

What each would cost:

**Question (Faces/bodies):** Should face shapes or more body sizes join the spec?

- **Neither:** Keep one face and one body per gender. Fastest path to Core deploy; less personal avatars.
- **Both now:** 3 face shapes and 3 body sizes per gender in C5. Most personal avatars; every garment refitted to 6 bodies, which pushes Core deploy back the most.
- **Face shapes only, never sizes:** Add 3 face shapes in C5 and keep body sizes out of scope for good.

**The issue with R34.** I re-checked both retention claims today:
- **Open-Meteo, 90 days: confirmed.** Their terms say "All log files will be deleted after a period of 90 days."
- **OpenStreetMap, 180 days: not supported for this app.** The OSMF privacy policy (last edited January 2026) gives 180 days only for Piwik, its analytics on OSM's own websites, which the app never loads. For server requests such as a Nominatim lookup, it says IP addresses and requests are logged and may be analysed to improve the map, but it gives no deletion period. Nominatim's usage policy just points to the OSMF policy.

**Question (OSM logs):** How should Info describe what OpenStreetMap keeps from a "Use my location" lookup?

- **State it accurately (Recommended):** "OpenStreetMap logs each lookup, including your IP address, under the OSMF privacy policy, which doesn't say how long these logs are kept." With a link to the policy. R34 is corrected to match, with the evidence recorded in Revisions.
- **Keep "180 days":** Matches the current spec, but the policy only gives 180 days for its website analytics, not for lookups, so Info would state something its source doesn't say.
- **Leave the period out:** Say only that OpenStreetMap receives the rounded coordinates and your IP address, and link the policy, without discussing retention.

That message tells us what's wrong. The app shows "This browser can't share your location here" only when the page isn't loaded over a secure (HTTPS) connection, or the browser has no location feature at all. Safari on iPhone does have location, so the likely cause is this:

**Your Home Screen icon was probably added from the Wi-Fi test address** (http://192.168.1.123:4173/…), not from the live site. That address is plain HTTP, and iOS only allows location on HTTPS pages. In Safari you've been using the live HTTPS site, which is why it works there.

**To confirm:** turn off Wi-Fi on your phone (stay on cellular) and open the Home Screen app. If it doesn't load, it's the Wi-Fi test copy, because that copy only works on your home network while my preview server is running.

**To fix it:**
1. Long-press the Quick Dresser icon → **Remove App** → **Delete from Home Screen**. This also removes that copy's saved avatar and location.
2. In Safari, go to **https://rg-sureshkumar-goat.github.io/weather-outfit-app/**. The address bar should show github.io, not 192.168…
3. Tap **Share** → **Add to Home Screen** → **Add**.

**Question (Body controls):** How should the body controls work on the review screen?

- **Stepped sliders (Recommended):** A slider for each body measure (build, height, plus bust and hips on the female body), each with 5–7 stops. Feels like fine-tuning, but every stop is a known value, so the extremes and middles can all be checked on the sample grid and every garment is guaranteed to fit.
- **Free sliders:** The same sliders with any value in between. The most personal, but endless combinations: fitting is checked on a sample, so an odd in-between mix could show a gap that no one has seen.
- **Named body types:** Pick one of about 6 body types per gender (for example slim, athletic, curvy, plus-size). The simplest and most reliable art, but the least personal: no separate height or bust and hip choices.

On your second point: noted. The type picker will be one list of tiles under the six group headings, each tile showing its icon and label like the icon sheet you saw. I'll record that in R17 and redraw P4c and L4 that way.

On your first point: the User can't reach the closet from the laptop. Everything is saved only on the device that added it:
- **Separate closets per device:** a phone and a laptop each have their own closet, avatar and location.
- **Same phone, two closets:** Safari and the Home Screen app keep separate storage too (that's in the AGENTS notes).
- **Why:** it was a deliberate privacy choice in the spec. R18 says photos are never uploaded, and R34 says there are no accounts, so the app can run as a static site on GitHub Pages with no server.

Sharing a closet across devices would mean changing that. Here are the realistic options.

**Question (Devices):** Should the closet be reachable from more than one device?

- **Keep it on one device (Recommended):** As the spec says now: no accounts, nothing uploaded, works on the static GitHub Pages site. Each device has its own closet, and Info says so plainly. No extra work in C7.
- **Add export and import:** A "Save my closet to a file" button on one device and "Load a closet file" on the other (photos included), moved by AirDrop, email or iCloud Drive. Still no accounts or server, but the transfer is manual and a one-time copy, not live sync. Adds a feature to C7 or a later checkpoint.
- **Accounts with cloud sync:** Sign in and the closet syncs everywhere. Needs a hosted backend (e.g. a database and file storage), accounts, uploading your photos, and rewriting R18, R34 and the Info privacy text. A large change in scope and in the app's privacy promise.

### Developer · 2026-10-11 23:29 CDT

**Should the closet be reachable from more than one device?** How much additional work would an account with cloud sync require, more importantly would it cost any money to setup a database or file storage system?

### Agent · 2026-10-11 23:29 CDT

Good questions; let me check current pricing rather than quote it from memory, since free tiers change. I'll load the web tools.

**Would it cost money?** Not for a project this size, if you pick the right service. Both main options have free tiers, checked on their pricing pages today:

| | Supabase Free | Firebase |
|---|---|---|
| Accounts | 50,000 monthly active users | 50,000 monthly active users (Spark, no cost) |
| Database | 500 MB | 1 GiB Firestore (Spark, no cost) |
| Photo storage | 1 GB, included in the free plan | **Not on the free Spark plan.** It needs the pay-as-you-go Blaze plan with a billing account (card on file). Blaze includes 5 GB stored free. |
| Catches | Free projects **pause after 1 week with no activity** (you unpause them); 2 free projects | You'd only pay past the free quotas, but a card has to be on file |
| Cheapest paid plan | Pro, $25/month | Pay as you go |

Supabase is the only one with free photo storage and no card. At a few hundred KB per photo, 1 GB holds a few thousand clothing photos, which is plenty for testing and your peers.

**How much extra work?** Roughly as much as C6a–C6d combined, so 3–4 checkpoints on top of the plan. It would cover:
- **Sign-in screens:** sign up, sign in, sign out and password reset (or email "magic links"), plus what happens before someone signs in.
- **Security rules:** so each person can only read and write their own closet and photos. Getting this wrong would expose other people's photos, so it needs careful testing.
- **Photo uploads and sync:** keeping the phone's local copy and the cloud copy in step, including adding an item on a train with no signal.
- **Moving data in:** bringing an existing on-device closet and avatar into a new account.
- **Delete all my data (R35):** this would have to delete the account and the server copies too.
- **Privacy rewrite:** R18, R34 and the Info privacy text all change, because photos would be uploaded to a third party. That needs new research on the service's data handling.
- **Setup you'd do yourself:** creating the project, and keeping the service's keys out of commits.

**My read:** it's doable for free with Supabase, but it's the biggest change in scope so far, and it reverses the app's "nothing leaves your device" promise. A middle path is export/import: no accounts, no cost, and you could add it later without blocking C7.

Which way do you want to go: keep it on one device, add export and import, or accounts with Supabase sync?

Sources:
- [Supabase pricing](https://supabase.com/pricing)
- [Firebase pricing](https://firebase.google.com/pricing)

### Agent · 2026-10-11 23:32 CDT

Here are the options, from least to most change:

| Option | How it works | Cost | Work | Privacy |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1. One device only** (the spec today) | Each device keeps its own closet. Info says so plainly. | Free | None | Nothing leaves the device |
| **2. Export and import** | "Save my closet to a file" on one device; "Load a closet file" on the other, moved by AirDrop, email or iCloud Drive. A one-time copy, not live sync. | Free | Small: about part of one checkpoint | Nothing uploaded by the app; you choose where the file goes |
| **3. Sync code, no accounts** | The app gives you a long private code. Enter it on another device and the closet syncs through a cloud database. No email or password. | Free on Supabase's free tier | Medium: about 2 checkpoints | Photos are uploaded, and anyone with the code can see the closet |
| **4. Accounts with cloud sync** | Sign in with email (or a magic link) on any device, and everything syncs automatically. | Free on Supabase (1 GB photos, pauses after a week idle); Firebase needs a card for photo storage | Large: about 3–4 checkpoints | Photos are uploaded to a third party; R18, R34 and the Info privacy text get rewritten, and Delete all my data must delete the account too |

Options 2 to 4 aren't mutually exclusive in time. For example, you could build C7 as one device only now, add export and import later, and decide on sync after usability testing.

Which do you want for now?

I can already explain why the checks were lost, from how C7 works:
- **Two colors at most:** the app samples the middle of the photo and keeps only the most common color (the base) and one clearly different color (the print). Several shades of blue get merged into one, and the white either becomes the print or is dropped.
- **No pattern shape:** the avatar draws every print the same way, as a small generic dash pattern in the print color. There's no notion of stripes, checks or dots yet.

Fixing it needs two things: more colors, and pattern types the avatar can actually draw. The options:

| | What it does | Reliability | Work |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Pattern choice + 3 colors** | Confirm step gets *Pattern: Solid · Stripes · Checks · Dots · Print* and up to 3 colors (e.g. navy, light blue, white), pre-filled from the photo. The avatar draws real stripes, checks or dots on every garment in those colors. | High: you pick the pattern, the app only suggests colors | Moderate: one set of pattern drawings works for all 34 garments |
| **2. Option 1 + automatic guess** | The app also guesses the pattern by looking for repeating color changes across the photo (both directions = checks, one = stripes), and you correct it if wrong. | Medium: folds, lighting and background confuse it, so it gets much better once the C8 cut-out removes the background | Option 1, plus a detection step that needs tuning on real photos |
| **3. A trained pattern model** | A machine-learning classifier for clothing patterns | Potentially best | No ready-made model exists for this; it would mean collecting and training our own, so it's out of scope |

My recommendation is Option 1 now, inside C7, so your shirt can be entered as Checks with its three colors. The automatic guess (Option 2) would come in C8, where the cut-out makes it reliable.

Which would you like, and can you copy the photo into `test-garments/` so I can test against it?

### Developer · 2026-10-12 10:36 CDT

I know that softwares such as photoshop have a clone stamp tool that can be used to copy patterns from one image onto another image, can we maybe add something like that where since the user gives you the type of garment they are entering, we add a basic template and then clone stamp the pattern from the inputed image onto the template the app creates in the background?

### Agent · 2026-10-12 10:37 CDT

Yes, that's doable, and it's a better fit than drawing generic patterns. What you're describing is texture mapping: take a patch of the real fabric from the photo and fill the garment's drawing with it. Here's how it would work:

1. **Pick the fabric.** On the confirm step, the photo shows a small square, like choosing the clone stamp's source point. The app places it in the middle of the item, and you can drag it onto a clean area of fabric, away from logos, buttons or background.
2. **Clean it up.** The patch is redrawn in its main colors (say navy, light blue and white), so it looks illustrated rather than photographic and keeps the avatar's cartoon style. Real folds and shadows in the photo would otherwise look odd on a drawn shirt.
3. **Fill the template.** The patch repeats across the garment's shape. Alternate copies are mirrored so the seams don't show, and it's scaled so the checks are roughly the right size on the avatar. The template's own folds, collar, buttons and shading are drawn on top, so it still reads as that garment.
4. **Store it with the item.** The patch is small (well under 100 KB) and stays on the device like the photo.

**Trade-offs to know about**
- **Plaids, stripes, dots and florals:** these work well, because they repeat.
- **Graphic tees** (a big logo or print): a repeated patch looks wrong. A "Use fabric from photo" switch handles this, off for those items and for plain solids.
- **Before the C8 cut-out:** the square has to be placed on fabric by hand. After C8 the app can place it automatically.
- **The rest of the app** still uses the base color for the color name, the "neutral" rule and the rack.
- **Starter clothes** stay flat colors.

**Scope:** about the same as the pattern-choice option, and it replaces it, since the real pattern makes a Stripes/Checks/Dots chooser unnecessary.

Since this changes how garments look, I'd prototype it first and show you your shirt on the avatar before building it into Add item. Copy the photo into `test-garments/` and I'll use it.

Shall I build the prototype?

### Developer · 2026-10-12 10:40 CDT

If we build this, is there any way we can sample the entire shirt instead of just a small patch of the shirt to capture the entire pattern or graphic of shirt?

### Agent · 2026-10-12 10:41 CDT

Yes. Instead of repeating a small patch, the app can stretch the whole photo of the shirt over the drawing, so a logo lands on the avatar's chest and the plaid lines up the way it does on the real shirt.

**How it would work**
1. **Find the shirt's outline in the photo.** This is what the C8 cut-out does: you tap the item and MediaPipe outlines it on the device, so the app knows exactly where the shirt ends and the background begins.
2. **Match key points.** For a top, those are the collar, shoulders, armpits and hem corners. Each point on the photo's outline is matched to the same point on the avatar's drawing, for whichever of the 12 bodies and pose is being drawn.
3. **Stretch the photo to fit.** The body panel is mapped onto the avatar's torso, and each sleeve onto each arm. Pants work the same way per leg, and dresses as one panel. The drawing's outline, collar and shading go on top, and the colors can be simplified so it still looks illustrated.

**Trade-offs**
- **Better:** graphics, logos and the exact pattern placement all come through, which a repeating patch can't do.
- **Needs the cut-out first:** without it, the app can't tell the shirt from the floor. So this belongs with C8, or C8 moves up.
- **Flat photos only:** a flat-lay photo shows only the front, and its wrinkles and folds come along unless they're smoothed out. A shirt photographed crumpled or at an angle will look stretched.
- **More work:** about twice the patch approach, because each kind of garment (tops, bottoms, dresses, shoes, accessories) needs its own point matching. Every garment and body type would also need re-checking in an art round.
- **Fallback:** for odd cases, like a photo with no clean outline, the repeating patch or the plain colors stay as the fallback.

**One decision: how to sequence it.**
- **A. Patch now, whole garment in C8 (my recommendation):** C7 gets the repeating fabric patch, so your plaid shirt works this week. C8 adds the cut-out and the whole-garment mapping, with the patch as the fallback. You get patterns now and graphics next.
- **B. Whole garment only, in C8:** C7 ships with plain colors. Patterns and graphics both arrive together in C8. Less total work, but nothing improves until C8.
- **C. Whole garment now, with a manual outline:** before C8, you drag a frame or corner points around the shirt on the photo yourself. It works sooner, but takes more effort per item and is less exact than the cut-out.

Which way do you want to go? Either way, copying your shirt photo into `test-garments/` lets me test with it.

### Agent · 2026-10-12 10:44 CDT

Yes, the app can do it automatically, with no tapping or outlining.

The cut-out tool planned for C8, MediaPipe's MagicTouch, normally needs a tap on the item to know which object you mean. The app can supply that tap itself:
1. **Take the middle point.** The camera screen already says "Lay the item flat", and its guide keeps the item centered, so the app uses the photo's center as the tap.
2. **Try a few nearby points as well,** and keep the best outline: one that covers a believable share of the photo and isn't just a logo or a shadow.
3. **Fall back quietly.** If no outline looks right (a cluttered background, or a white shirt on a white sheet), the app still saves the item with its colors, and offers to retake the photo on a plainer background. It never asks the user to trace anything.

It all runs on the phone, as planned, so nothing is uploaded.

**What I'd want to prove first:** how well the automatic outline handles real clothing photos. That means patterned and multicolor items, white on light backgrounds, and shirts with sleeves spread out. That's exactly where your own photos in `test-garments/` would help.

**This also simplifies the plan.** The patch idea needed the user to place a square, which is the kind of extra step you want to avoid. So my recommendation is:
- **Commit C7 now as it is:** plain base and print colors, every flow tested.
- **Make C8:** automatic cut-out, then the whole garment mapped onto the avatar's drawing, with an automatic repeating patch for odd cases. Spec R16 changes from "the User taps the item" to "the app finds it". The C8 plan and P4b wireframe get updated, and the step count goes back to 3.

## C8: the app cuts the garment out by itself

**Proposed split.** C7 was split in two, and I'd do the same here, with a checkpoint and commit after each part:
- **C8a, the cut-out:** cut-out, colors, storage, model loading.
- **C8b, the photo look on the avatar:** the whole-garment mapping, with an art round.

### C8a: automatic cut-out (R15, R16, R17, R18, R34, R37)

**Model**
- I'll download `magic_touch.tflite` once and commit it to `public/models/`, as the plan says. It's 6,227,884 bytes, Apache 2.0, from Google's `storage.googleapis.com/mediapipe-models/interactive_segmenter/magic_touch/float32/1/`.
- MediaPipe 1.0.1 has a newer segmenter that expects a different, larger model. C8 uses the original keypoint version (`InteractiveSegmenterLegacy`), which matches the 6.2 MB model R16 names.

**Loading**
- The model and runtime start loading when Add item opens, with a progress line.
- Today never imports the model code.

**Cut-out, with no user step**
- The app tries the center of the photo and 8 nearby points, so pants laid flat still work when the center lands between the legs.
- It scores each result: covers the middle, a plausible size, one main shape. It keeps the best one and cleans the edges.

**Colors**
- Colors are sampled from inside the cut-out. The center and edge guess from C7 stays as the fallback.

**Flow**
- Still 3 steps. The "tap to cut out" screen (P4b) goes away.
- The cut-out runs while the User picks the type. The confirm step shows the result.

**Fallback**
- If there's no usable cut-out, the item is saved with colors from the photo.
- The confirm step shows one line suggesting a retake laid flat on a plain background in good light, with a Retake button. Saving is never blocked.

**Storage**
- Each item keeps its cut-out (R18). The Closet tiles and detail show the cut-out.
- Items saved in C7 keep their photo.

### C8b: the photo look on the avatar (new R44, art round)
- **How it fits:** key points per type family.
  - Tops and overgarments: the torso plus each sleeve.
  - Pants, shorts and suit pants: the waist plus each leg.
  - Skirts and dresses: one piece.
- **Look:** each region is mapped onto the matching part of the drawing and posterized to about 8 colors. The drawing's outline, shading and folds go on top. Add a `texture` option next to `print` in `piece()` in `src/art/draw.js`.
- **Patch fallback:** shoes, hats and scarves are too small to map, so they get a repeating patch. Any garment whose key points can't be found gets the same. Glasses keep colors only.
- **Where it shows:** Today, the Add item preview and the Closet rack.

### Agent · 2026-10-12 10:59 CDT

I plan to use three Google models, all from Google's MediaPipe. All three run on the phone or laptop and are served from the app's own site. The app never sends a photo to Google or anywhere else.

| Model | Used for | Checkpoint | Size | License |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **MagicTouch** (Interactive Segmenter), `magic_touch.tflite`, float32 version 1 | Cutting the garment out of the photo | C8 | 6.2 MB | Apache 2.0 |
| **Face Landmarker**, `face_landmarker.task` | Reading skin tone and face position from the selfie | C9 | 3.8 MB | Apache 2.0 |
| **Hair Segmenter** | Reading hair color and length from the selfie | C9 | 0.8 MB | Apache 2.0 |

- **The face landmarker file holds three smaller models:** face detection, a 478-point face mesh and expressions. It finds where the face's features are; it doesn't recognize who someone is, and Google's model card says so.
- **They also need Google's MediaPipe runtime** (about 11.8 MB). That's the engine that runs the models, not a model itself. It comes from the `@mediapipe/tasks-vision` npm package already installed (version 1.0.1) and is shared by all three models.
- **When they load:** the selfie models load only during setup, and MagicTouch only on Add item (R37). Today never loads any of them.

**Not using:**
- Google's newer MagicTouch (int8, 30.5 MB). It's five times larger, and R16 specifies the original.
- Gemini or any other cloud model. They would need the photos uploaded, and the free tier doesn't produce images.

I checked the MagicTouch file's size on Google's storage today without downloading it: 6,227,884 bytes. The other two sizes come from research.md and the feasibility test on 2026-10-03.

Before the summary, I need to know how the garment drawings are built: which parts are the torso, the sleeves and the legs, and where a pattern could hook in.

Here's the C8b summary. Nothing is built yet.

## C8b: the item's own pattern on the avatar (new R44, art round)

**Goal:** your flannel's plaid shows on the avatar's shirt, in its four recognized colors, with the drawing's outline, shading and folds still on top.

**How it works**
1. **Pattern map.** When an item is saved, the app turns its cut-out into a pattern map: each pixel is matched to the nearest of the item's recognized colors, then speckle is smoothed out so it reads as drawn, not photographed. Stored with the item: about 50–100 KB, on the device only.
2. **Whole-garment fit.** The cut-out's outline box is stretched onto the drawing's box for the whole garment: torso and sleeves together for tops, waist and legs together for bottoms, the whole piece for skirts and dresses. A shirt on a hanger or laid flat has its parts roughly where the avatar has them, so this covers most photos without finding collars and cuffs.
3. **Gap filling.** Where the photo has nothing behind a part of the drawing (for example, sleeves laid out sideways), a repeating patch of the pattern fills in. The patch also covers shoes, hats and scarves, which are too small to map. Glasses keep their frame color only.
4. **Recoloring.** Changing a color swatch recolors the pattern. For example, white to yellow turns the flannel's white stripes yellow.
5. **No cut-out, no change.** Items without a cut-out keep today's base-and-print look.
6. **Where it shows:** Today, the Add item preview and the Closet rack.
7. **Older items.** Items saved in C8a without a pattern map get one the first time they're shown.

**Art round:** I'll show the flannel and drawn test items on both bodies and several body types: a striped tee, a tee with a chest graphic, a plaid skirt, a printed dress, jeans and sneakers. Each comes in two or three levels of detail, from flat drawn colors to closer to the photo. You pick the look before anything is committed.

**Files**
- **New:**
  - `src/vision/pattern.js`: builds the pattern map, the outline box and the patch.
  - `tests/pattern.test.js`.
- **Changed:**
  - `src/art/draw.js`: `piece()` gets the pattern fill under the shading and lines.
  - `src/art/avatar.js`: places each item's pattern over its whole garment.
  - The garment files, if any piece needs its part named.
  - `src/store/closet.js`, `src/main.js`.
  - `src/screens/add.js`, `today.js`, `closet.js`: preview, Today and rack.
  - `src/art/artPage.js`: drawn test patterns only. Your flannel never goes into the repo; it appears only in screenshots I show you.
- **Documents:**
  - spec.md: new R44, plus R17 (recoloring) and R18 (pattern stored), with a Revisions entry.
  - plan.md: C8b.
  - AGENTS.md: art notes.
  - The P4d note: the avatar wears the item's pattern.

**Proof**
- `npm test` passes.
- Art-round sheets for your approval.
- The flannel on Today and in the Add item preview at 375 px and 1280 px.
- Changing a swatch recolors the pattern.
- An item without a cut-out looks unchanged.
- Today still loads no model, and draws quickly with several patterned items.

**Risks**
- **Odd photos.** Folded or angled photos won't line up with the drawing; the patch hides the gaps, but some will look smeared.
- **Graphics.** A chest graphic may stretch with the shirt, or repeat inside the patch.
- **Muddy blends.** Your flannel's gray-blue blend areas may look muddy once flattened. The art round will show it.
- **Speed.** Today has to rebuild the pattern images each visit. I'll cache them and time it.

If the box-stretch fit lines up badly in the art round, the fallback is to find the torso and sleeves separately, one garment type at a time. That's more work and more fragile, so I'd start with the box.

Go ahead with C8b on this plan?

### Agent · 2026-10-12 16:29 CDT

Pricing changes often, so I'll check current OpenAI pricing and policies rather than answer from memory.

Most of these results are third-party sites. Checking OpenAI's own pricing page:

OpenAI's own page lists prices per token, not per image, and mentions no free tier. Checking their image-generation guide for per-image costs:

I have OpenAI's official per-image prices. One more fact to check before answering: what OpenAI keeps from images sent to its API, since that bears on your privacy rule.

**Short answer: using ChatGPT would cost money, and it would break the app's on-device privacy promise. I'd try a free collar fix first.**

**Cost** (checked on OpenAI's own pricing pages today):
- **No free tier.** OpenAI's pricing page lists no free tier or free credits for image models, so every image is billed.
- **Per image (GPT Image 2, 1024×1024):** $0.006 at low quality, $0.053 at medium, $0.211 at high. Putting a garment on an avatar is an edit with input photos, and those input images are billed on top, at $8 per million tokens. That's roughly $0.05–0.25 per image at the usable quality levels.
- **Per item, once (cleanup only):** you could send each garment photo once when it's added and have it redrawn as a clean, flat, front-view shirt, which the warp then fits. About $0.05–0.10 per item at medium quality, so roughly $5–10 for 100 items.
- **Per outfit (full try-on):** a new image every time the outfit changes (each day, each Shuffle, each forecast day) could be $0.50–2.50 a day per person. You would be paying for every user.
- **The ChatGPT app itself** (the chat website or a ChatGPT plan) can't be called by Quick Dresser. Only the paid API can.

**What it would change in the project:**
- **A server.** The API key can't sit in a GitHub Pages app, because anyone could copy it and spend on your account. It needs a small server to hold it. The free option from research.md is a Cloudflare Worker. You'd also set a spending limit in OpenAI's dashboard.
- **Privacy.** Garment photos would leave the phone, contradicting R16, R18, R34 and the Info text ("item photos never leave the device"). OpenAI says API data isn't used for training by default, but it's kept for up to 30 days for abuse monitoring.
- **Speed and consistency:**
  - Generation takes seconds up to about 2 minutes.
  - The model redraws rather than copies, so the plaid's layout or a logo can shift.
  - Generating the whole dressed avatar would also redraw its face and body each time, losing your customizer.

**Free fix for the collar:** on collared shirts, draw the avatar's own collar on top of the photo, filled with the shirt's actual fabric. The drawn collar always lines up with the neck, because it's part of the drawing. The rest of the shirt stays your photo. It stays on the device and costs nothing.

**Question (Collar fix):** How do you want to fix the collar?

- **Free fix first (Recommended):** Draw the avatar's own collar on top, filled with the shirt's real fabric. On the device, no cost, about an hour. I show you the result next to the current one, and if it still looks off we revisit AI.
- **AI cleanup per item:** Each garment photo goes to OpenAI once when added and comes back as a clean, flat shirt to fit. About $0.05–0.10 per item, plus a small server for the key. Photos leave the device, so R16, R18, R34 and Info change. Adds several checkpoints.
- **Explore AI try-on further:** I write up a fuller comparison of AI services (OpenAI, Google, Cloudflare, open models) for garment try-on, with costs and privacy, before any build decision. No code changes yet.

### Agent · 2026-10-12 16:49 CDT

You're right. The gray patches come from how the B map works: it recolors your photo pixel by pixel. Where the photo is hazy or blended (the shoulders under the hanger's light, the navy-and-white crossings, the hem), pixels turn into the gray-blue color, so large areas lose the plaid.

A plaid is a pattern that repeats across the whole shirt. So instead of recoloring the photo in place, the app can cut a clean square of the plaid from the middle of the body and repeat it across the entire drawing, at the shirt's real scale. Every part of the drawing then gets real plaid, including the shoulders, collar and hem.

Rendering that for the flannel now to compare:

**Question (Repeat or fit):** Should the app repeat the pattern like this? Repeating suits all-over patterns (plaid, stripes, checks, florals), but a shirt with one placed graphic, like a logo on the chest, would get that logo repeated all over.

- **Choose automatically (Recommended):** The app compares the pattern's colors across the shirt. If they're spread evenly (plaid, stripes, all-over prints), it repeats a clean square. If one area differs (a chest graphic), it fits the map instead, so the graphic stays in place. Plain items look the same either way.
- **Always repeat:** Every patterned item repeats a square of its pattern. Simplest, and always fills cleanly, but a placed graphic gets copied across the shirt.
- **Repeat, with an override:** Repeat by default, plus a "Pattern: Repeat / Placed" switch on the confirm step and in Edit for items with a graphic. One optional tap when needed.

That settles the design. Here's the spec for the new C8b. Please check it before I plan the build.

**Goal:** each closet item with a cut-out appears on the avatar in the app's 2D style, like the hand-drawn flannel, whenever the app can read its pattern. Otherwise it appears as the photo fit. The app decides for each item, and the User can switch it.

**What the app decides, on the device, after the cut-out in Add item:**
1. **One main color:** Drawn, flat in its colors.
2. **A woven repeat it reads with confidence** (stripes, plaid, check): Drawn. It reads the stripe order, widths and colors, and draws them at the garment's real scale, straight across the body and along sleeves and legs. Where colors cross, it draws the twill, and a light brushed texture covers the fabric.
3. **Anything else** (a graphic, logo or floral, or an unsure reading): Photo fit.
4. **Details on button shirts:** chest pockets (none, one or two, with or without flaps) and the button color. When unsure, it uses the type's standard drawing.

**For the User:**
- **"Show as: Drawn / Photo":** on the confirm step and in Edit, preset by the app. Drawn without a pattern reading means flat base and print colors.
- **Color edits (R17):** they change the matching stripes in Drawn. Photo always shows the photo's own colors.

**Stored on the device (R18):**
- The reading (pattern, stripes, scale, details) and the Show as setting.
- Today draws Drawn items from the stored reading, with no photo processing.

**Replaced:** the earlier color map, repeat check, dots switch and recoloring (R44). The photo fit stays as the fallback.

**Constraints:**
- Nothing is uploaded (R16, R18, R34), and Today loads no vision code (R37).
- It works on all 34 types and 12 body types.
- The reading's pure parts are unit-tested.
